respectively. Throughout the manuscript, whenever the state is a thermal state at temperature
\beta
, we use the shorthand notation
\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}
and
S(\beta)
.

The non-equilibrium free energy bears a close relation to the
relative entropy
,

D(\rho\|\sigma)=\textup{tr}(\rho\ln\rho)-\textup{tr}(\rho\ln\sigma).

(51)

Whenever
\rho
and
\sigma
are diagonal in the same basis, the relative entropy can be written as

D(\rho\|\sigma)=\sum_{i}p_{i}\ln\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}},

(52)

where
p_{i},q_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
\rho
and
\sigma
respectively. Now, for any Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, consider
\tau_{\beta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\hat{H}}/Z_{\beta}
, which is the thermal state at some inverse temperature
\beta
, with partition function
Z_{\beta}=\textup{tr}[\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\hat{H}}]
, and denote its eigenvalues as
q_{i}
. Then for any diagonal state
\rho
with eigenvalues
p_{i}
, and denoting
\{E_{i}\}_{i}
as the eigenvalues of
\hat{H}
,

\displaystyle D(\rho\|\tau_{\beta})=\sum_{i}p_{i}\ln\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}=-S(\rho)+\sum_{i}p_{i}(\beta E_{i}+\ln Z_{\beta})=\beta F(\rho)+\ln Z_{\beta}.

(53)

This implies that

F(\rho)=\frac{1}{\beta}[D(\rho\|\tau_{\beta})-\ln Z_{\beta}].

(54)

In Section
D
we will solve Eq. (
49
) in order to evaluate the maximum efficiency.

B.2
Second laws for nanoscopic systems

In the microscopic quantum regime, where only a few quantum particles are involved, it has been shown that macroscopic thermodynamics is not a complete description of thermodynamical transitions. More precisely, not only the non-equilibrium free energy, but a whole other family of generalized free energies have to decrease during a state transition
[
7
]
. This places further constraints on whether a particular transition is allowed.
In particular, these laws also give necessary and sufficient conditions, when a system with initial state
\rho_{\textup{ColdW}}^{0}
can be transformed to final state
\rho_{\textup{ColdW}}^{1}
(both diagonal in the energy eigenbasis), with the help of any catalyst/machine which is returned to its initial state after the process.

Formally, these laws correspond to the following case:
A transition from the initial state
\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
to the final state
\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
, is possible iff there exists an energy-preserving unitary
U(t)
on the global system, (i.e. a unitary that obeys
[U(t),\hat{H}_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}]=0
), where states
\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
,
\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
are of the form Section described in
A
(i.e. the state of the machine
\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0}
is preserved).
If
(\tau_{\textup{Hot}}^{0},\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}})
and
(\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0},\hat{H}_{\textup{M}})
can be arbitrarily chosen, these correspond to the set of
catalytic thermal operations

[
7
]
one can perform on the joint state
ColdW
. This implies that the cold bath is used as a resource state.

We can apply these second laws to our scenario by associating the catalyst with
\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}}
, and considering the state transition
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\rightarrow\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
as described in Section
A
. Note that the initial state
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
is block-diagonal in the energy eigenbasis (for the battery by our choice, and for the cold bath because it is a thermal state). By catalytic thermal operations, the final state is also block-diagonal in the energy eigenbasis. Furthermore, according to the second laws in
[
7
]
, the transition from
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\rightarrow\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is then possible iff

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}},\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\geq F_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\otimes\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}},\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\quad\forall\alpha\geq 0,

(55)

where
\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h}
is the thermal state of the system at temperature
T_{\textup{Hot}}
of the surrounding bath. The quantity
F_{\alpha}(\rho,\sigma)
for
\alpha\geq 0
corresponds to a family of free energies defined in
[
7
]
, which can be written in the form

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(\rho,\tau)=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)-\ln Z_{h}\right],

(56)

where
D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)
are known as
\alpha
-Rényi divergences. Sometimes we will use the short hand
F_{\infty}:=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}F_{\alpha}
. On occasion, we will refer to a particular transition as being possible/impossible according to the
F_{\alpha}
free energy constraint. By this, we mean that for that particular value of
\alpha
and transition, Eq. (
55
) is satisfied/not satisfied. The
\alpha
-Rényi divergences can be defined for arbitrary quantum states, giving us necessary (but insufficient) second laws for state transitions
[
7
,
13
]
. However, since we are analyzing states which are diagonal in the same eigenbasis, the Rényi divergences can be simplified to

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\ln\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha}q_{i}^{1-\alpha},

(57)

where
p_{i},

q_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
\rho
and the state
\tau
. The cases
\alpha=0
and
\alpha\rightarrow 1
are defined by continuity, namely

\displaystyle D_{0}(\rho\|\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0^{+}}D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)=-\ln\sum_{i:p_{i}\neq 0}q_{i},

(58)

\displaystyle D_{1}(\rho\|\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 1}D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)=\sum_{i}p_{i}\ln\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}},

(59)

and we also define
D_{\infty}
as

\displaystyle D_{\infty}(\rho\|\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty^{+}}D_{\alpha}(\rho\|\tau)=\ln\max_{i}\frac{p_{i}}{q_{i}}.

(60)

The quantity
D_{1}(\rho\|\tau)
coincides with
D(\rho\|\tau)
, as we have defined in Eq. (
51
), and evaluated in Eq. (
52
) for diagonal states. We will often use this convention.
Furthermore, since we are considering initial states which are block-diagonal in the energy eigenbasis, these generalized second laws are both necessary and
sufficient
conditions for state transformations.
Therefore, in Section
E.2.1
we will solve Eq. (
55
) explicitly to find an expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}
with the ultimate goal of evaluating the maximum efficiency in this regime.

The reader should note that for both Section
B.1
and
B.2
, the conditions for state transformation place upper bounds on the quantity
W_{\textup{ext}}
. In particular, this allows us to express the maximum values
W_{\textup{ext}}
can take (such that the joint state transformation of cold bath and battery is possible) in terms of quantities related to the cold bath, and the error probability
\varepsilon
. It is also worth comparing the conditions for state transformation in Section
B.1
and
B.2
, which are stated in Eqs. (
49
) and (
55
). In particular, Eq. (
49
) is but a particular instance of Eq. (
55
), and therefore the nanoscopic second laws always place a stronger upper bound on
W_{\textup{ext}}
compared to the macroscopic second law.

Appendix C
Efficiency, maximum efficiency and how to evaluate it

The central quantity of interest in this article is the efficiency of heat engines. Since we have already introduced the notion of a heat engine in Section
A
, and the rules which govern the possibility of thermodynamical transitions of one cycle of a heat engine in Section
B
, it is timely to define the efficiency. After defining this quantity, we demonstrate how to go about calculating its maximum value under different conditions, such as for perfect work, near perfect work, in both the macroscopic and nanoscopic regimes. This will prepare the scene for Sections
D
and
E
, where we evaluate the maximum efficiency more explicitly.

C.1
Definition of efficiency and maximum efficiency

As stated in the main text, the efficiency of a particular heat engine (recall that a heat engine is defined by its initial and final states
\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{0},\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{1}
as described in Section
A
) is defined as

\eta:=\frac{W_{\rm ext}}{\Delta H},

(61)

where
W_{\rm ext}
is the amount of work extracted which is defined in Eq. (
43
), and
\Delta H
is the amount of mean energy drawn from the hot bath, namely
\Delta H:=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}}\rho_{\textup{Hot}}^{0})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}}\rho_{\textup{Hot}}^{1})
, where
\rho_{\textup{Hot}}^{1}
is the reduced state of the hot bath.

Now, consider the set of conditions on state transformations given by Eq. (
55
) for nanoscale systems.
As discussed in Section
B
, these conditions place a restriction on the range of values
W_{\textup{ext}}
can take. Therefore, for any fixed
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
, we define
\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
as the maximum achievable efficiency as a function of the final state of the cold bath. More precisely,

\displaystyle\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})

(62)

\displaystyle=\sup_{W_{\textup{ext}}}\eta(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})\quad\textup{ subject to }\quad F_{\alpha}(\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0},\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\geq F_{\alpha}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1},\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\quad\forall\alpha\geq 0.

(63)

In Eq. (
62
), we have written the quantity in Eq. (
61
) as
\eta=\eta(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
to remind ourselves of its explicit final cold bath state dependency.
Therefore, the maximum efficiency will correspond to maximizing over the final state of the cold bath:

\eta_{\textup{max}}=\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}),

(64)

where
\mathcal{S}
is the space of all quantum states in
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
.
By analyzing this quantity in Section
E
, we show that perfect work cannot be extracted. Therefore, when we calculate the maximization in Eq. (
64
) we will consider near perfect work (see Def.
5
).

In the macro regime, we have to satisfy a less stringent requirement, namely the macroscopic second law of thermodynamics. And hence we have that for fixed
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
,
\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
is the maximum efficiency as a function of
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}

\displaystyle\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=\sup_{W_{\textup{ext}}}\eta(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ subject to }\quad F(\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}})\geq F(\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}})

(65)

\displaystyle\textup{ and }\quad\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{t}\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{0})=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{t}\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{1}),

(66)

where
\hat{H}_{t}
is defined in Eq. (
37
).
Similarly to the nanoscale setting, the maximum efficiency is

\eta_{\textup{max}}=\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}).

(67)

We can also define the maximum quasi-static efficiencies for the macro and nano scale. The maximum efficiency of a quasi-static heat engine (see Def.
6
), is

\displaystyle\eta_{\textup{max}}^{\textup{stat}}=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\tau(g)),

(68)

\displaystyle\eta_{\textup{max}}^{\textup{stat}}=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\tau(g)),

(69)

for the
nanoscopic
and
macroscopic
cases respectively.
\tau(g)\in\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
is the thermal state with Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
at temperature
\beta_{f}=\beta_{c}-g
and
\eta^{\textup{nano}},\eta^{\textup{mac}}
are defined in Eqs. (
62
) and (
65
) respectively.
Since we can extract perfect and near perfect work in the macroscopic setting, we will derive the efficiency for both cases in Section
D
.

C.2
Finding a simplified expression for the efficiency

We can find a more useful expression for
\Delta H
appearing in Eq. (
61
). This can be obtained by observing that since only energy preserving operations are allowed, we have

\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{t}\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{0})=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{t}\rho_{\textup{ColdHotMW}}^{1}),

(70)

where
\hat{H}_{t}=\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}}+\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}+\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}+\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}
. Since the Hamiltonian does not contain interaction terms between these systems, the mean energy depends only on the
reduced states
of each system. Mathematically, it means that Eq. (
70
) can be written as

\displaystyle\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}}\rho_{\textup{Hot}}^{0})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0})=

(71)

\displaystyle\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Hot}}\rho_{\textup{Hot}}^{1})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rho_{\textup{M}}^{1})+\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}).

(72)

Also, note that since
\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0}=\rho_{\textup{M}}^{1}
, therefore
\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0})=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rho_{\textup{M}}^{1})
. This implies that we have

\Delta H=\Delta C+\Delta W,

(73)

where

\displaystyle\Delta C

\displaystyle:=\textup{tr}\left[\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\right]-\textup{tr}\left[\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\right],

(74)

and

\displaystyle\Delta W

\displaystyle:=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}).

(75)

are the change in average energy of the cold bath and battery. We can thus write Eq. (
61
) as

\eta=\frac{W_{\textup{ext}}}{\Delta W+\Delta C}.

(76)

Furthermore, from Eqs. (
41
), (
42
), (
43
) and (
75
), we have
\Delta W=(1-\varepsilon)W_{\textup{ext}}
, and hence we can write the inverse efficiency as

\eta^{-1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=1-\varepsilon+\frac{\Delta C(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})}{W_{\textup{ext}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})},

(77)

where we have made explicit the
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
dependency. We already know from the setting that
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
is thermal. If
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is also a thermal state at some temperature
\beta
according to the cold bath Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
, we will sometimes use the shorthand notation
\eta(\beta)
for
\eta(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})
and
\Delta W(\beta)
,
\Delta C(\beta)
for
\Delta W(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})
,
\Delta C(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})
respectively.

In Section
D
, we will derive an expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}
and solve Eqs. (
65
), (
67
). In Section
E
, we will derive a new expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}
in the nanoscopic regime, and solve Eqs. (
62
), (
64
).

Appendix D
Efficiency of a heat engine according to macroscopic thermodynamics

In this section, we study the efficiency of the setup detailed in Section
A
under the constraints of macroscopic thermodynamics, as described in Section
B.1
. This implies that the non-equilibrium free energy solely dictates whether
\rho_{\textup{ColdW}}^{0}\rightarrow\rho_{\textup{ColdW}}^{1}
is possible. We find that in both cases of extracting perfect and near perfect work,

(1)

The maximum achievable efficiency is the Carnot efficiency.

(2)

The Carnot efficiency can be achieved for any cold bath Hamiltonian.

(3)

For any
\Delta C
, the maximum efficiency achievable for the particular value of
\Delta C
, is achieved iff the final state of the cold bath is thermal (according to a different temperature
T_{f}
).

(4)

When the final state of the cold bath is thermal, the Carnot efficiency is achieved iff we take the limit corresponding to a quasi-static heat engine (Eq. (
69
)). Roughly speaking, this means that there is only infinitesimal change in the final temperature of the cold bath, compared to its original state.

This section can be summarized as follows: in Section
D.1
, we first apply the macroscopic law of thermodynamics, namely the fact that non-equilibrium free energy is non-increasing, to our heat engine setup. By making use of energy conservation, we can derive the amount of maximum extractable work as shown in Eq. (
81
). Next, in Section
D.2
we show that when considering the extraction of perfect work, we show the points (1)-(4) as stated above. In Section
D.3
, we show that points (1)-(4) hold also when considering near perfect work.

The main results can be found in Theorem
1
and Lemma
6
.
One may think points (1)-(4) are obvious since it has long been known that the optimal achievable efficiency of a heat engine operating between two thermal baths is the Carnot efficiency, and that this efficiency can be achieved quasi-statically. The motivations for proving these results here are two-fold. Firstly, this is a rigorous and mathematical proof of optimality, while usually one encounters arguments such as reversibility, or that the heat engine must remain in thermal equilibrium at all times during the working of the heat engine. Secondly, we will find later on at the nano/quantum scale that the Carnot efficiency can be achieved but observation (2) does not hold anymore. For these reasons, it is worthwhile proving that one can actually achieve points (1)-(4) in this setting for any cold bath Hamiltonian according to macroscopic thermodynamics. From a practical point of view, many of the technical results proved here will be needed in the proofs of Section
E
, where we derive results involving a more refined set of generalized free energies, which describes thermodynamic transitions for nanoscale quantum systems.

D.1
Maximum extractable work according to macroscopic law of thermodynamics

Our first task is to find an expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}
in the macro regime. We do so by solving Eq. (
49
) for
W_{\textup{ext}}
such that

\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{ColdMW}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}S(\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}})\leq\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{ColdMW}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}S(\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}}).

(78)

The entropy is an additive quantity under tensor product, meaning that
S(\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2})=S(\rho_{1})+S(\rho_{2})
for any states
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
. Furthermore, since the joint Hamiltonian does not contain interaction terms, therefore the mean energy also depends only on the reduced states. In summary, both
S
and
\langle\hat{H}\rangle
are additive under a tensor product structure of
\rho^{0}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
and
\rho^{1}_{\textup{ColdMW}}
as described in Eqs. (
38
) and (
39
). This means one can rewrite Eq. (
78
) by expanding its terms,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{M}}}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[S(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})+S(\rho^{1}_{\textup{M}})+S(\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}})\right]\leq

(79)

\displaystyle\qquad\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}}}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[S(\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}})+S(\rho^{0}_{\textup{M}})+S(\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}})\right],

Furthermore, note that
\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0}=\rho_{\textup{M}}^{1}
, and therefore
S(\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0}),\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{M}}\rangle_{\rho_{\textup{M}}^{0}}
are common terms on both sides of Eq. (
79
) which can be cancelled out. Furthermore, by our construction of the battery in Eqs. (
40
)-(
43
), we have that
S(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0})=0
,
S(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1})=\Delta S=\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon)
and
\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}}=E_{j}^{\textup{W}}
and
\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}}=(1-\varepsilon)E^{W}_{k}+\varepsilon E^{W}_{j}
. Thus, Eq. (
79
) can be simplified to

(1-\varepsilon)W_{\rm ext}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}S(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})\leq\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}S(\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}})+\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon),

(80)

where
W_{\rm ext}
has been defined in Eq. (
43
). In other words,
(1-\varepsilon)W_{\textup{ext}}\leq F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0})-F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon)
.

We can also express
W_{\text{ext}}
with the
relative entropy
instead, by using Eq. (
54
).
We can apply this identity to Eq. (
80
) whenever the initial and final states are diagonal in the energy eigenbasis. Note that the initial
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
is a thermal state (of some temperature), and therefore diagonal in the energy eigenbasis. Since we start with a state
\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\otimes\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}
which is diagonal w.r.t. the Hamiltonian, and since catalytic thermal operations can never increase coherences between energy eigenstates (or in the macro setting, since we only demand mean energy conservation), we know that the final state
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\otimes\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}
is also diagonal in the energy eigenbasis.
Therefore, Eq. (
80
) can be rewritten w.r.t. the relative entropies as follows

(1-\varepsilon)W_{\rm ext}\leq F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0})-F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})+\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon)\right].

(81)

D.2
Maximum efficiency for perfect work is Carnot efficiency

In this section, we want to find the maximum efficiency according to Eqs. (
61
), (
65
) and (
67
), for the case of
\varepsilon=0
which implies
\textup{h}_{2}(\varepsilon)=0
.
We do this by the following steps:

1.

Evaluate
W_{\textup{ext}}
.
According to Eq. (
81
), we know that

W_{\rm ext}=F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0})-F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})\right],

(82)

where recall that we have defined
\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h}
previously as the thermal state of system
Cold
with temperature
T_{\textup{Hot}}
. Note that here equality can be achieved because in macroscopic thermodynamics, satisfying the free energy constraint is a necessary and sufficient condition for the possibility of a state transformation.
Note that since by construction the initial and final states of the battery are pure energy eigenstates, namely
\varepsilon=0
and therefore

W_{\rm ext}=\Delta W.

(83)

2.

Write inverse maximum efficiency as optimization problem.
By substituting the simplified expression for efficiency derived in Eq. (
77
) into Eq. (
67
), we have

\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}=\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}(\eta^{\rm mac})^{-1}=1+\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}.

(84)

3.

Maximize
W_{\textup{ext}}
given a fixed value of
\Delta C
.
This is done in Lemma
1
, where we show that given a fixed
\Delta C
, the final cold bath state that maximizes
W_{\textup{ext}}
is uniquely a thermal state, corresponding to a certain inverse temperature
\beta^{\prime}
.

4.

Show that 3) implies that efficiency is maximized by a thermal state of the cold bath.
This is proven in Lemma
2
. Therefore, this implies one only needs to optimize Eq. (
84
) over one variable, i.e.
\beta_{f}
, the final temperature of the cold bath.

5.

Show that the efficiency is strictly increasing with
\beta_{f}
.
This is done first by proving several identities, which are summarized in Corollary
1
. Using these identities, we prove in Lemma
4
that the first derivative of efficiency w.r.t.
\beta_{f}
is always positive over the range where
W_{\textup{ext}}>0
. This leads us to conclude, in Theorem
1
, that maximum efficiency is achieved in the limit
\beta_{f}\rightarrow\beta_{c}
, and evaluating the efficiency at this limit gives us the Carnot efficiency.

Firstly, let us develop a technical Lemma
1
, which concerns the unique solution towards maximizing
W_{\textup{ext}}
for a fixed
\Delta C
. By applying Lemma
1
, we show in Lemma
2
that the maximal efficiency is achieved when
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is a thermal state. The reader can easily find similar proofs in
[
64
]
.

Lemma 1
.

Given any Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
, a corresponding thermal state
\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h}
of some temperature
\beta_{h}
, and a fixed initial state
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
, consider the maximization over final states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
,

\max_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}W_{\textup{ext}}\,=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-\min_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})\right].

(85)

over all states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
which are diagonal in the energy eigenbasis, subject to the constraint that
\Delta C
is a constant. Then the solution for
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is unique, and
\rho
is a thermal state according to the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
at a certain temperature
\beta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Firstly, from Eq. (
74
) we see that the constraint
\Delta C
being a constant, is the same as
\textup{tr}\left[\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\right]
being a constant. This is because they differ only by a constant term.
On the other hand, from Eq. (
75
) and (
83
), we can see that
\max_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}W_{\textup{ext}}
is equal to the R.H.S. of Eq. (
85
).
Since
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
and
\tau
are both diagonal in the energy eigenbasis (
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
by the statement in the lemma, and
\tau
by it being a thermal state), one can evaluate the relative entropy by using Eq. (
51
). Denote the eigenvalues of our variable
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
to be
\{p_{i}\}_{i}
, and the eigenvalues of the thermal state
\tau
to be
\{q_{i}\}_{i}
. We can then write the optimization problem as

\displaystyle\min_{\{p_{i}\}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}(\ln p_{i}-\ln q_{i});\quad\text{ subject to }\sum_{i}p_{i}E_{i}=c\quad{\rm constant,and}\penalty\ \sum_{i}p_{i}=1.

\displaystyle{\rm where}\quad q_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}};\quad Z_{\beta}=\sum_{i}e^{-\beta E_{i}}.

We can now employ techniques of Lagrange multipliers to solve this optimization. The constrained Lagrange equation is

\displaystyle L(\{p_{i}\},\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i}(\ln p_{i}-\ln q_{i})+\lambda\left(\sum_{i}E_{i}p_{i}-c\right)+\mu\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}-1\right),

(86)

\displaystyle\frac{dL}{dp_{i}}

\displaystyle=(\ln p_{i}-\ln q_{i}+1+\lambda E_{i}+\mu)=0,

(87)

\displaystyle\frac{dL}{d\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}E_{i}p_{i}-c=0.

(88)

\displaystyle\frac{dL}{d\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i}-1=0.

(89)

We find that the normalized solution is

\displaystyle p_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta^{\prime}E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta^{\prime}}},\quad Z_{\beta^{\prime}}=e^{(1+\mu)}Z_{\beta},

(90)

and
p_{i}
are probabilities corresponding to the Boltzmann distribution, according to inverse temperature
\beta^{\prime}=\beta+\lambda
. Depending on the mean energy constraint
c
and normalization condition, one can solve for the Lagrange multipliers
\lambda
and
\mu
. With this we conclude that the state
\rho
which maximizes
D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau)
is a thermal state, where its temperature is such that the constraint on mean energy is satisfied.
∎

Lemma 2
.

Consider the work extraction process described by the state transformation
\rho_{\textup{ColdMW}}^{0}\rightarrow\rho_{\textup{ColdMW}}^{1}
, where
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
,
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}
and
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}
have been described in Section
A
. Denote
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
as the Hilbert space of the cold bath. Then the maximal efficiency in Eq. (
84
) is obtained for a final state of the cold bath
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
, which is thermal:

\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}=1+\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\tau}}\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}},

(91)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\tau}
the set of all thermal states (for
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
with any temperature
T>0
) in
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
. Furthermore, all non-thermal states do not achieve the maximum efficiency, i.e.

\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}<1+\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}\Big|_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\quad\text{ for any }\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{\tau}.

(92)

where
\mathcal{S}
is the space of all quantum states in
\mathcal{H}_{\textup{Cold}}

Proof.

First of all, note that without loss of generality we can always consider only diagonal states, as explained in the paragraph before Eq. (
81
) that catalytic thermal operations do not increase coherences between energy eigenstates.
We begin by substituting Eqs. (
74
) and (
82
) into Eq. (
84
), and finding

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}

\displaystyle=1+\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}

(93)

\displaystyle=1+\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{\beta_{h}\Delta C}{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})}

(94)

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\left[\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})}{\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c})}\right]^{-1}.

(95)

In the last line of Eq. (
95
), we see that only two terms depend on the maximization variable
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
. This means we can perform the maximization in two steps:

\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})}{\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c})}=\sup_{A>0}\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-B(A)}{A}

(96)

where
B(A)
is the optimal value of a separate minimization problem:

B(A)=\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}\\
\textup{tr}(H_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c})=A\end{subarray}}D_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})

(97)

From Lemma
1
, we know that the solution of the sub-minimization problem in Eq. (
97
) has a unique form, namely
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}=\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{f}
is a thermal state of some temperature
\beta_{f}
. Therefore, Eq. (
96
) can be simplified to

\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})}{\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c})}=\sup_{\beta_{f}}\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{f}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})}{\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{f})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c})}.

(98)

Whats more, for every constant
A
, the function

f(x)=\left(1+\beta_{h}\left[\frac{D_{1}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{c}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-x}{A}\right]^{-1}\right)^{-1}

(99)

is bijective in
x\in{\mathbbm{R}}
and thus due to the uniqueness of the sub-minimization problem in Eq. (
97
), we conclude that for all non-thermal states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
, the corresponding efficiency will be strictly less than that of Eq. (
95
).
Thus from Eq. (
98
) and (
95
) we conclude the lemma.
∎

After establishing Lemma
2
, we can continue to solve the optimization problem in Eq. (
84
) by only looking at final states which are thermal (according to some final temperature
\beta_{f}
which we optimize over). In the next Lemma
3
and Corollary
1
, we derive some useful and interesting identities. These identities concern quantities such as the derivatives of mean energy and entropy of the thermal state (with respect to inverse temperature), and relates them to the variance of energy. The reader can find similar proofs in any standard thermodynamic textbook (For example in Sections 6.5, 6. of
[
60
]
), but we derive them here for completeness.

Lemma 3
.

For any cold bath Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
, consider the thermal state
\tau_{\beta}=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}}
with inverse temperature
\beta
. Define
\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta})
, and
S(\beta)=-\tau_{\beta}\ln\tau_{\beta}
to be the mean energy and entropy of
\tau_{\beta}
. Then the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=-{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta}

(100)

\displaystyle\frac{dS(\beta)}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=-\beta\cdot{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta},

(101)

where
{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta}=\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}^{2}\rangle_{\beta}-\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}^{2}
is the variance of energy for
\tau_{\beta}
.

Proof.

Intuitively we know that the expectation value of energy increases as temperature increases (or as the inverse temperature decreases). More precisely, consider the probabilities of
\tau_{\beta}
for each energy level of the Hamiltonian
E_{i}
,

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}},\quad{\rm where}\penalty\ Z_{\beta}=\sum_{i}e^{-\beta E_{i}}

\displaystyle\frac{dp_{i}}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}^{2}}\left[-E_{i}e^{-\beta E_{i}}\cdot Z_{\beta}-\frac{dZ_{\beta}}{d\beta}\cdot e^{-\beta E_{i}}\right]=-p_{i}E_{i}-\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\frac{dZ_{\beta}}{d\beta}p_{i}=-p_{i}E_{i}+p_{i}\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}.

(102)

The last equality holds because of the following identity:

\frac{-1}{Z}\frac{dZ}{d\beta}=\frac{-1}{Z}\sum_{i}(-E_{i})e^{-\beta E_{i}}=\sum_{i}p_{i}E_{i}=\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}.

(103)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}}{dp_{i}}\frac{dp_{i}}{d\beta}=\sum_{i}E_{i}\cdot\left[-p_{i}E_{i}+p_{i}\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}\right]

(104)

\displaystyle=-\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}^{2}\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}^{2}=-{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta}.

(105)

On the other hand, similarly, one can prove the second identity by writing down the expression of entropy for the thermal state,

\displaystyle S(\beta)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}}\ln\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}}=\sum_{i}\beta E_{i}\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}}+\ln Z_{\beta}\sum_{i}\frac{e^{-\beta E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta}}=\beta\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}+\ln Z_{\beta}.

(106)

Therefore, the derivative of
S(\beta)
w.r.t.
\beta
is

\displaystyle\frac{dS(\tau_{\beta})}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}+\beta\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}}{d\beta}+\frac{1}{Z_{\beta}}\frac{dZ_{\beta}}{d\beta}=\beta\cdot\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}}{d\beta}=-\beta\cdot{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta}.

(107)

∎

By using Lemma
3
in a special case, we obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 1
.

Given any Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
, consider the quantities

\displaystyle\Delta C(\beta_{f})=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta_{f}})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta_{c}})=\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{f}}-\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{c}}

(108)

and

\displaystyle W_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})=F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-F(\tau_{\beta_{f}})=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\right],

(109)

where
\tau_{\beta}
corresponds to the thermal state defined by
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
at inverse temperature
\beta
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta C(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle=-{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}

(110)

\displaystyle\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{h}-\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}.

(111)

Proof.

For
\Delta C(\beta_{f})
, it is straightforward from Lemma
3
that

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta C(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}=\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{f}}}{d\beta_{f}}=-{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}.

(112)

On the other hand,
\Delta W(\beta_{f})
can be simplified by substituting Eq. (
54
) into Eq. (
109
),

\displaystyle W_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})

\displaystyle=F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-F(\tau_{\beta_{f}})=\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{c}}-\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{f}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[S(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-S(\tau_{\beta_{f}})\right].

(113)

With this, we can evaluate the derivative

\displaystyle\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{f}}}{d\beta_{f}}+\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\frac{dS(\tau_{\beta_{f}})}{d\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle={\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}-\frac{\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{h}-\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}.

The second equality is obtained by Lemma
3
for
\frac{d\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta_{f}}}{d\beta_{f}}
, and the third by grouping common factors together.
∎

In the next step, by using Corollary
1
, we show that when the final state of the cold bath is thermal, the optimal efficiency is achieved only in the quasi-static limit, i.e. in the limit
\beta_{f}\rightarrow\beta_{c}
when the efficiency is optimised over all final thermal states of the cold bath.

Lemma 4
.

Evaluate the efficiency expressed in Eq. (
77
) for the situation where the final state of the cold bath is a thermal state at inverse temperature
\beta_{f}
:

\eta(\beta_{f})=\frac{W_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})}{\Delta C(\beta_{f})+W_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})}.

(114)

Then for all
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
,
\frac{d\eta(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}>0.

Proof.

To prove this, we show that
\frac{d\eta^{-1}}{d\beta_{f}}<0
, where
\eta^{-1}=1+\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}
. Evaluating the derivative of
\eta^{-1}
w.r.t.
\beta_{f}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d\eta^{-1}}{d\beta_{f}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{W_{\textup{ext}}^{2}}\cdot\left[\frac{d\Delta C(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}W_{\textup{ext}}-\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}(\beta_{f})}{d\beta_{f}}\Delta C\right]

(115)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}}{W_{\textup{ext}}^{2}}\cdot\left[-W_{\textup{ext}}-\frac{\beta_{h}-\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}\Delta C\right]

(116)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}}{W_{\textup{ext}}^{2}}\cdot\left[\Delta C+\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}\left[S(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-S(\tau_{\beta_{f}})\right]-\frac{\beta_{h}-\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}\Delta C\right]

(117)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}}{W_{\textup{ext}}^{2}}\frac{\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}}\cdot\left[\Delta C-\frac{1}{\beta_{f}}[S(\tau_{\beta_{f}})-S(\tau_{\beta_{c}})]\right].

(118)

The first equality is obtained by invoking the chain rule of differentiation. The second equality is obtained by substituting
\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}}{d\beta_{f}},\frac{d\Delta C}{d\beta_{f}},
as evaluated earlier in Corollary
1
. The third equality is obtained by expressing
W_{\textup{ext}}
according to Eq. (
113
), plus recognizing that
\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\tau_{\beta_{f}}}-\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\tau_{\beta_{c}}}=\Delta C
. The last inequality is obtained, simply by taking out a common term
\beta_{f}/\beta_{h}
. We then make the following observations:

1) The factor

\frac{\beta_{f}}{\beta_{h}W_{\textup{ext}}^{2}}>0,

(119)

2) The variance of energy for any positive temperature

{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}>0,

(120)

3) and the last term
\Delta C-\frac{1}{\beta_{f}}[S(\tau_{\beta_{f}})-S(\tau_{\beta_{c}})]
can be written as
F(\tau_{\beta_{f}})-F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})
, where
F
is the free energy of a system w.r.t. a bath with inverse temperature
\beta_{f}
. But then, since
\tau_{\beta_{f}}
is the thermal state with the same inverse temperature, this means that
\tau_{\beta_{f}}
is the
unique
state that minimizes free energy. Therefore,
F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-F(\tau_{\beta_{f}})>0
for any
\tau_{\beta_{c}}
.

∎

From Lemma
2
and Lemma
4
, we conclude that the maximization of efficiency for any Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
happens for a final state which is thermal, and the greater its inverse temperature
\beta_{f}
, the higher efficiency is. With these lemmas we can now prove the main result of this section (Theorem
1
).

In the next theorem, we evaluate the efficiency at the limit
\beta_{f}\rightarrow\beta_{c}^{-}
, and show that it corresponds to the Carnot efficiency.

Theorem 1

(Carnot Efficiency)
.

Consider all heat engines which extract perfect work (see Definition
4
). Then according to the macroscopic second law of thermodynamics, the maximum achievable efficiency (see Eq. (
67
)) is the Carnot efficiency

\eta_{\textup{max}}=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}}.

(121)

It can be obtained for
all
cold bath Hamiltonians
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
, but when the final state of the cold bath
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}
is thermal, then
only
for quasi-static heat engines (as defined in Def.
6
and Eq. (
69
) for quasi-static maximum efficiency). In this quasi-static limit, an infinitesimal amount of work is extracted.

Proof.

From Eq. (
67
), we have an expression for the optimal efficiency in terms of a maximization over final cold bath states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}
. By Lemma
2
, we know that the optimal solution is obtained only for thermal states. Subsequently, by Lemma
4
, it is shown that when the final cold bath is of temperature
\beta_{f}
, the corresponding efficiency is strictly increasing w.r.t.
\beta_{f}
.
Also note that since by definition
W_{\rm ext}>0
, this implies that
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
. Intuitively, this is because heat cannot flow from a cold to hot system without any work input. One can also see this mathematically, by showing that for any
\beta\geq\beta_{h}
,

\displaystyle\frac{dF(\tau_{\beta})}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\beta}\left[\langle\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rangle_{\beta}-\frac{1}{\beta_{h}}S(\beta)\right]=\left(\frac{\beta}{\beta_{h}}-1\right){\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta}\geq 0.

(122)

This implies that if
\beta_{f}\geq\beta_{c}\geq\beta_{h}
, then
F(\beta_{f})\geq F(\beta_{c})
, and according to Eq. (
109
)
W_{\textup{ext}}\leq 0
.
Therefore, when the final state of the cold bath
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is thermal, the optimal efficiency must be achieved only when its inverse temperature
\beta_{f}
approaches
\beta_{c}
from below. Let
\beta_{f}=\beta_{c}-g
, where
g>0
. Then we have

\eta_{\textup{max}}^{-1}=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}(\eta^{\textup{mac}})^{-1}(\beta_{c}-g),\quad(\eta^{\textup{mac}})^{-1}(\beta_{c}-g)=1+\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}\Big|_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}=\tau_{(\beta_{c}-g)}}.

(123)

Since as
g\rightarrow 0^{+}
, both the numerator and denominator vanish, we can evaluate this limit by first applying L’Hôspital rule, the chain rule for derivatives (for any function
F
,
\frac{dF}{dg}=-\frac{dF}{d\beta_{f}}
), and then Corollary
1
to obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\frac{d\Delta C}{dg}}{\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}}{dg}}=\lim_{\beta_{f}\rightarrow\beta_{c}^{-}}\frac{\frac{d\Delta C}{d\beta_{f}}}{\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}}{d\beta_{f}}}=\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}-\beta_{h}}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathrm{max}}^{-1}=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}(\eta^{\textup{mac}})^{-1}(\beta_{c}-g)=1+\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}-\beta_{h}}=\frac{\beta_{c}}{\beta_{c}-\beta_{h}}

(124)

and hence
\eta_{\mathrm{max}}=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}}
.
∎

D.3
Maximum efficiency for near perfect work is still Carnot efficiency

In this section, we show that even while allowing a non-zero failure probability
\varepsilon>0
in the near perfect work scenario, the maximum achievable efficiency is still the Carnot efficiency. It is worth noting that this result is also important later, as an upper bound to maximum efficiency in the nanoscopic regime. We first prove it in Lemma
5
for the case where the final state of the battery is fixed as in Eq. (
42
). Then later, we show in Lemma
6
that Carnot efficiency is still the maximum, even if we allow a more general final battery state. Before we present the proof, it is useful for the reader to recall the definition of near perfect work (Def.
5
) and quasi-static heat engines (Def.
6
).

Lemma 5
.

Consider all heat engines which extract near perfect work (see Def.
5
).
Then according to the macroscopic second law of thermodyanmics, the maximum efficiency of a heat engine,
\eta_{\textup{max}}
is the Carnot efficiency

\eta_{\textup{max}}=\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}},

(125)

and the supremum is achieved for quasi-static heat engines (see Def. (
6
) and Eq. (
69
)).

Proof.

The ideas in this proof are very similar to that of Section
D.2
, and the main complication comes from proving that even if we allow
\varepsilon>0
, as long as
\Delta S/W_{\textup{ext}}
is arbitrarily small, the maximum efficiency cannot surpass the Carnot efficiency.

Let us begin by establishing the relevant quantities for near perfect work extraction. The amount of work extractable from the heat engine, when we have a probability of failure, according to the standard free energy can be obtained by solving Eq. (
81
).
We thus have that the maximum
W_{\rm ext}
is

W_{\textup{ext}}=\beta_{h}^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\left[D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S\right],

(126)

where
\Delta S
is defined in Eq. (
45
).

Before we continue with the analysis, we will note a trivial consequence of Eq. (
126
). Condition 1) in Def
5
implies that
(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}
is upper bounded. The terms in square brackets in Eq. (
126
) are also clearly upper bounded for finite
\beta_{c},\beta_{h}
. Hence
W_{\textup{ext}}
is bounded from above.
\Delta S
is solely a function of
\varepsilon
and only approaches zero in the limits
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
,
\varepsilon\rightarrow 1^{-}
; and
\varepsilon\rightarrow 1^{-}
is forbidden by 1) in Def
5
. Thus if 1) and 2) in Def
5
are satisfied,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta S}{W_{\textup{ext}}}=0.

(127)

In turn, if Eq. (
127
) is satisfied, then we have near perfect work by Def.
5
. Thus Eq. (
127
) is satisfied iff we have near perfect work. We will use this result later in the proof.

Extracting a positive amount of near perfect work implies that we can rule out all states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
such that
D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\leq D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
from the analysis. This can be proven by contradiction: if
D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\leq D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
, then from Eq. (
126
)
\beta_{h}W_{\textup{ext}}\leq\Delta S/(1-\varepsilon)
and together with 2) in Def
5
this would imply

0<\beta_{h}(1-\varepsilon)\leq\frac{\Delta S}{W_{\textup{ext}}}<p.

(128)

However, since from 1) Def.
5
we have
\varepsilon\leq l
, Eq. (
128
) cannot be satisfied for all
p>0
, leading to a contradiction.

From Eq. (
77
) we have

\eta_{\textup{max}}^{-1}=1-\varepsilon+\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{\Delta C}{W_{\textup{ext}}}=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left[1+\frac{\beta_{h}\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S}\right],

(129)

where
\Delta C=\Delta C(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
and is defined in Eq. (
74
).

Firstly, let us show that with a similar analysis as shown in Lemma
2
, the maximum efficiency occurs when
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is a thermal state. From Eq. (
129
), we have

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)\left[1+\beta_{h}\inf_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S}\right]

(130)

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)\left[1+\beta_{h}\inf_{A>0}\frac{A}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-B(A)+\Delta S}\right]

(131)

where

\displaystyle B(A)=\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}\\
\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta_{c}})=A\end{subarray}}D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}}).

(132)

We can split this minimization problem to Eqs. (
131
) and (
132
) because
D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
and
\Delta S
do not depend on the variable
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
. Furthermore, when
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is a thermal state of inverse temperature
\beta_{f}
, we have seen in the beginning of the proof in Theorem
1
that for
W_{\textup{ext}}>0
,
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
. This implies that the variable
A=\Delta C=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta_{f}})-\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}\tau_{\beta_{c}})>0
.

By Lemma
1
, for any fixed
A>0
we conclude that the infimum in Eq. (
132
) is achieved
uniquely
when
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is a thermal state. Therefore, our optimization problem is simplified to optimization over final temperatures
\beta_{f}
(or
g=\beta_{c}-\beta_{f}
),

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left[1+\beta_{h}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{f}\\
\Delta C>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S}\right]

(133)

Consider cases of
\beta_{f}
, where
D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
is non-vanishing (finite), i.e. which are not
quasi-static
. Note that this always corresponds to extracting near perfect work, since when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, we have
\varepsilon,\Delta S\rightarrow 0
and these contributions disappear from Eq. (
133
). However, by Lemma
2
we also know that the infimum over
\beta_{f}
occurs uniquely at the quasi-static limit, when
g\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

What remains, is then to consider the quasi-static heat engine, namely the limit
g\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Extracting near perfect work in this case corresponds to requiring that
\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta S}{W_{\textup{ext}}}=0
, where
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(g)
and
\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\varepsilon(g)=0
. Equivalently

\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{W_{\textup{ext}}}{\Delta S}=\infty.

(134)

Substituting Eq. (
126
) into Eq. (
134
),

\displaystyle\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}(1-\varepsilon(g))^{-1}\left[1+\frac{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}{\Delta S}\right]=\infty

(135)

which implies that
\displaystyle\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}{\Delta S}=\infty,
or equivalently,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta S}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}=0.

(136)

Finally, we evaluate the inverse efficiency at the quasi-static limit,

\displaystyle\eta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}(1-\varepsilon(g))\cdot\left[1+\beta_{h}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S}\right]

(137)

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S}

(138)

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta C}{\left[D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\right]}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\Delta S}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}\right)^{-1}

(139)

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{d\Delta C(\tau_{\beta_{f}})/dg}{dD(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})/dg}

(140)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{h}-\beta_{c}},

(141)

where from Eq. (
139
) to (
140
), we make use of Eq. (
136
) : the second term within the limit is simply 1, and the first term depends only on
g
, which we can obtain Eq. (
140
) by invoking the L’Hôspital rule.
The last equality in Eq. (
141
) follows directly from the identities we derived for
\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}}{d\beta_{f}}
and
\frac{d\Delta C}{d\beta_{f}}
in Corollary
1
,

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta C}{dg}

\displaystyle=-\frac{d\Delta C}{d\beta_{f}}=-{\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}}

(142)

\displaystyle\frac{dD(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}{dg}

\displaystyle=-\frac{dD(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}{d\beta_{f}}=\beta_{h}\frac{dW_{\textup{ext}}}{d\beta_{f}}=(\beta_{h}-\beta_{f}){\rm var}(\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}})_{\beta_{f}},

(143)

while in the limit
g\rightarrow 0,\penalty\ \beta_{f}=\beta_{c}
.

Finally, we now see that the quasi-static efficiency is

\eta=\left(\frac{\beta_{h}-\beta_{c}-\beta_{h}}{\beta_{h}-\beta_{c}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{\beta_{c}-\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}}=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}}

(144)

which is exactly the Carnot efficiency.
∎

Later, in Section
F.2
we will need Lemma
2
to hold in a more general scenario, i.e. instead of the final battery state being
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}=(1-\varepsilon)\left|E_{k}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{k}\right|_{\textup{W}}+\varepsilon\left|E_{j}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{j}\right|_{\textup{W}}
, we want to allow the final battery state to be any energy block-diagonal state with trace distance
\varepsilon
. Next we state and prove this generalized lemma.

Lemma 6
.

Consider all heat engines which extract near perfect work (see Definition
5
), but allowing for any final battery state with a trace distance
\varepsilon
to the ideal final pure state
\left|E_{k}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{k}\right|_{\textup{W}}
.
Then according to the macroscopic second law of thermodynamics, the maximum efficiency of a heat engine,
\eta_{\textup{max}}
is the Carnot efficiency

\eta_{\textup{max}}=\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}},

(145)

and when the final state of the cold bath
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}
is thermal, the supremum is
only
achieved for quasi-static heat engines (see Def. (
6
) and Eq. (
69
)).

Proof.

Firstly, let us note that since the initial state
\rho_{\textup{ColdW}}^{0}
which we start out with is energy block-diagonal, the final state has to also be block-diagonal. Therefore, given the product structure between the cold bath and battery, it is sufficient to consider the case when the final battery state is energy block-diagonal. Next, let us note that any final state
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{2}
which is energy block-diagonal, and has trace distance
\varepsilon
with
\left|E_{k}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{k}\right|_{\textup{W}}
can be written as,

\rho_{\textup{W}}^{2}=(1-\varepsilon)\left|E_{k}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{k}\right|_{\textup{W}}+\varepsilon\rho_{\textup{W}}^{\textup{junk}},\penalty\ \textup{where }\rho_{\textup{W}}^{\textup{junk}}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\left|E_{i}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{i}\right|_{\textup{W}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{i}p_{i}=1\penalty\ \textup{and}\penalty\ p_{k}=0.

(146)

Next, one can calculate
W_{\textup{ext}}
given by the standard free energy condition, i.e.

F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})+F(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0})\geq F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+F(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{2}).

(147)

Using the identity
F(\rho)=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}\rho)-\beta^{-1}S(\rho)
, we have that

\displaystyle F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})+E_{j}

\displaystyle\geq F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+(1-\varepsilon)E_{k}+\varepsilon\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{\textup{junk}})-\beta^{-1}_{h}S(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{2}).

(148)

Substituting
W_{\textup{ext}}=E_{k}-E_{j}
, and rearranging terms, we have

(1-\varepsilon)W_{\textup{ext}}\leq F(\tau_{\beta_{c}})-F(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})+\beta^{-1}_{h}\Delta S-\varepsilon[\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{\textup{junk}})-E_{j}].

(149)

Finally, by using the identity (in Eq. (
54
)) that
F(\rho)=\beta^{-1}_{h}[D(\rho\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-\ln Z_{\beta_{h}}]
, the maximum amount of extractable work is given by

W_{\textup{ext}}=(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}\beta^{-1}_{h}\cdot[D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S-\varepsilon\tilde{E}],

(150)

where
\tilde{E}=\textup{tr}(\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}\rho_{\textup{W}}^{\textup{junk}})-E_{j}
.

Following the steps in Lemma
5
, in particular the derivations in Eq. (
130
) and (
131
), we have

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm max}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left[1+\beta_{h}\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{f}\\
\Delta C>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S-\varepsilon\tilde{E}}\right].

(151)

To show Eq. (
151
) gives the Carnot efficiency, we show that 1) for non quasi-static cases where
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
, Carnot efficiency is not attained, and 2) in the quasi-static limit, Carnot efficiency is attained.

Let us first consider the case of extracting a non-vanishing amount of near perfect work, i.e. for all cases where
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
. Then near perfect work, by Def.
5
, corresponds to the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\eta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\penalty\ (1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left[1+\beta_{h}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})+\Delta S-\varepsilon\tilde{E}}\right]

(152)

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\frac{\Delta C}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}.

(153)

In this limit, all terms involving
\varepsilon
vanish, and the inverse efficiency has the same expression as the efficiency for perfect work. We already know from Lemma
4
that the infimum over
\beta_{f}
cannot be obtained in this regime, since the inverse efficiency is strictly decreasing with
\beta_{f}
.

Therefore, again we are left with analyzing the quasi-static limit for this problem. Following the derivation in Eq. (
139
) for the quasi-static limit, we obtain

\displaystyle\eta^{-1}_{\rm max}

\displaystyle=1+\beta_{h}\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta C}{\left[D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\right]}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\Delta S-\varepsilon\tilde{E}}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}\right)^{-1},

(154)

where
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(g)
and note that requiring near perfect work implies that

\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\Delta S}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}=0.

(155)

Next, we observe the relationship between
\varepsilon
and
\Delta S
, in the regime where
\varepsilon
is small. Given any
\varepsilon>0
denoting the trace distance
d(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{2},\left|E_{k}\right\rangle\!\!\left\langle E_{k}\right|_{\textup{W}})=\varepsilon
, the smallest amount of entropy that can be produced corresponds to
\Delta S=h_{2}(\varepsilon)
. This is because if we try to distribute the weight
\varepsilon
over more energy eigenvalues, then by majorization the entropy only increases. But we also know that
\varepsilon\leq h_{2}(\varepsilon)
for small values of
\varepsilon
, in particular over the regime
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Therefore, we have that in this regime,
\varepsilon\leq h_{2}(\varepsilon)\leq\Delta S
holds.
Therefore, we also know that

\lim_{g\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\varepsilon\tilde{E}}{D(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})}=0,

(156)

where
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(g)
. Plugging Eqns. (
155
) and (
156
) into Eq. (
154
), we have that the quasi-static efficiency is
\eta=1-\frac{\beta_{h}}{\beta_{c}}
.
∎

Appendix E
Efficiency of a nanoscopic quantum heat engine

In this section, we will be applying the conditions for state transitions for nanoscale systems, as detailed in Section
B.2
. The reader will see that due to these extra constraints from the generalized free energies, the fundamental limitations on efficiency will differ greatly from those observed in Section
D
.

Firstly, in Section
E.1
, we show that the extraction of a positive amount of perfect work is
impossible
using the setup. In Section
E.2
, we show that this can be resolved by considering near perfect work instead. Then we find that:

(1)

The maximum achievable efficiency is still the Carnot efficiency. This is proven in Section
E.2.2
.

(2)

However, in the case of quasi-static heat engines, the Carnot efficiency
cannot
be achieved for all cold bath Hamiltonians.
This is our main result, which is stated in Theorem
2
, found in Section
E.2.6
.
The results in Section
E.2.4
and
E.2.5
are more technical proofs, that pave the way for deriving this main result.

E.1
Impossibility of extracting perfect work

We will first show that with the general setup as described in Section
A
, no perfect work can ever be extracted. By this we mean that whenever
\varepsilon
as defined in Eq.(
42
) equals zero, then for any value of
W_{\rm ext}>0
, and for any final state
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
, the transition
|E_{j}\rangle\langle E_{j}|_{\textup{W}\!}\otimes\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\rightarrow|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|_{\textup{W}\!}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
is not possible. Intuitively speaking, this occurs because the cold bath is initially in a state of full rank. Since thermal operations cannot decrease the rank of the system, therefore the final state of the cold bath
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
must also be of full rank. By directly solving Eq. (
55
), for
W_{\rm ext}
, we can find the an equation governing the amount of extractable work. To solve Eq. (
55
) for
W_{\rm ext}
, we start by first using Eq. (
56
) to find an expression solely in term of the Rényi divergences and the fact that the bipartite thermal states are product states,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\geq D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\otimes\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau_{\textup{ColdW}}^{h})\quad\forall\alpha\geq 0.

(157)

Now using the additivity of the Rényi divergences, from (
157
) it follows

D_{\alpha}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h})-D_{\alpha}(\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h})\leq D_{\alpha}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})\quad\forall\alpha\geq 0.

(158)

By directly solving the L.H.S., we find that
W_{\rm ext}
must satisfy

\displaystyle W_{\rm ext}\leq kT_{\textup{Hot}}\left[D_{\alpha}(\tau^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{Cold}})-D_{\alpha}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{Cold}})\right]\quad\forall\alpha\geq 0.

(159)

Hence

\displaystyle W_{\rm ext}\leq kT_{\textup{Hot}}\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\left[D_{\alpha}(\tau^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{Cold}})-D_{\alpha}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{Cold}})\right],

(160)

where
\tau^{h}_{\textup{Cold}}
is the thermal state of the cold bath (according to the cold bath Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
), at temperature
T_{\textup{Hot}}
(since the surrounding hot bath is of temperature
T_{\textup{Hot}}
). However from Eq. (
56
),
D_{0}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})=D_{0}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})
. Therefore according to Eq. (
160
), the amount of work extractable satisfies
W_{\rm ext}\leq 0
.

We phrase this with more rigor in the following Lemmas
7
and
8
, which proves that for perfect work,
W_{\textup{ext}}>0
is impossible. The proof holds for general initial states
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
of full rank, in particular, they need not even be diagonal in the energy eigenbasis.

Lemma 7
.

For any
W_{\rm ext}>0
, consider the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}
given by Eq. (
40
). Then for any inverse temperature
\beta_{h}>0
, the thermal state
\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}=\frac{1}{\textup{tr}[e^{-\beta_{h}\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}}]}e^{-\beta_{h}\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}}
satisfies

\textup{tr}\left[\left(|E_{j}\rangle\langle E_{j}|_{\textup{W}}-|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|_{\textup{W}}\right)\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}\right]>0.

(161)

Proof.

Follows directly from the definitions. Since
W_{\rm ext}>0
, we know that
E_{j}^{\textup{W}}<E_{k}^{\textup{W}}
. Evaluating the quantity above gives
\frac{1}{\textup{tr}[e^{-\beta_{h}\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}}]}\cdot\left(e^{-\beta_{h}E_{j}^{\textup{W}}}-e^{-\beta_{h}E_{k}^{\textup{W}}}\right)>0
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

Consider any general quantum state
\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}
of full rank. Then for any
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}
, the transition from
\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}\otimes\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\rightarrow\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\otimes\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}
is not possible via catalytic thermal operations if

\textup{tr}\left[\left(\Pi_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}}-\Pi_{\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}}\right)\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}\right]>0,

(162)

where
\Pi_{\rho}
is the projector onto the support of state
\rho
, and
\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}
is the thermal state of the battery at the initial hot bath temperature.

Proof.

One can show this by invoking the quantum second law for
\alpha=0

[
7
]
, which says that if
\rho_{\rm in}\rightarrow\rho_{\rm out}
is possible via catalytic thermal operations, then

D_{0}(\rho_{\rm in}\|\tau)\geq D_{0}(\rho_{\rm out}\|\tau),

(163)

where
\tau
is the thermal state of the system at bath temperature, and

\displaystyle D_{0}(\rho\|\sigma)=\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\ln\textup{tr}[\rho^{\alpha}\sigma^{1-\alpha}]=-\ln\textup{tr}[\Pi_{\rho}\sigma],

(164)

is defined for arbitrary quantum states
\rho,\sigma
.
Applying this law with
\rho_{\rm in}=\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
and
\rho_{\rm out}=\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
, we arrive at

D_{0}(\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{W}})-D_{0}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}\|\tau^{h}_{\textup{W}})\geq D_{0}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})-D_{0}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h}),

(165)

where
\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h}
and
\tau^{h}_{\textup{W}}
are thermal states of the cold bath and battery at the temperature of surrounding hot bath (
T_{\textup{Hot}}
) respectively.
Since
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
have full rank, and since
\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h}
is normalized, therefore according to Eq. (
164
),
D_{0}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{h})=0
. Furthermore, since the
\alpha-
Rényi divergence
D_{0}
is non-negative, therefore the r.h.s. of Eq. (
165
) is lower bounded by 0. Thus, we have

\textup{tr}[(\Pi_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}}-\Pi_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}})\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}]\leq 0.

(166)

Since this is a necessary condition for state transformations, we arrive at the conclusion that: when Eq. (
166
) is violated, state transformations are not possible. But from Lemma
7
, any type of perfect work extraction violates Eq. (
166
). Therefore, in this setting, perfect work extraction is always impossible.

∎

To summarize, Lemma
8
implies that if the initial state of the cold bath is thermal, and therefore of full rank, then any work extraction scheme via thermal operations bringing
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}=|j\rangle\langle j|_{\textup{W}}
to
\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}=|k\rangle\langle k|_{\textup{W}}
where
W_{\rm ext}=E_{k}^{\textup{W}}-E_{j}^{\textup{W}}>0
is not possible. In general, we see that if
\Pi_{\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}}\neq\Pi_{\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}},
then when transition
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}
to
\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}
is possible, transition
\rho^{1}_{\textup{W}}
to
\rho^{0}_{\textup{W}}
is not.
Consequentially, we will have to consider near perfect work at the nano regime.

E.2
Efficiency for extracting near perfect work

As we have just seen in the previous Section
E.1
, we cannot extract perfect work. Due to the impossibility result, we consider the relaxation of extracting near perfect work in the nanoscale setting.

•

We begin by evaluating the expression for efficiency according to the nanoscopic laws of thermodynamics, given a final state of the cold bath, and comparing it to the expression according to macroscopic laws of thermodynamics. This is done in Sections
E.2.1
and
E.2.2
, and the relation between two efficiencies are summarized in Eq. (
177
). Since the nanoscopic efficiency is always smaller than the macroscopic efficiency, which attains Carnot efficiency only in the limit
\Delta C\rightarrow 0
, it will be a necessary condition to consider this limit if we want to achieve the Carnot efficiency, when considering nanoscopic laws of thermodynamics.

•

We analyze the quasi-static regime, focusing on the special case where the cold bath consists of
n
qubits. Since the quasi-static limit corresponds to the case of small
g>0
, and
\varepsilon
also has to be arbitrarily small for near perfect work extraction, we perform Taylor expansion of the analytical expressions for
W_{\textup{ext}}
and
\Delta C
w.r.t.
g
and
\varepsilon
. This is done in Section
E.2.3
.

•

In Section
E.2.4
, we identify how to choose
\varepsilon(g)
such that it corresponds to drawing near perfect work in the quasi-static limit. We first begin by observing that any continuous function
\varepsilon(g)
that vanishes in the limit
g\rightarrow 0
can be characterized with a real-valued parameter
\bar{\kappa}
that determines how quickly
\varepsilon
goes to zero. This is shown in Lemma
11
. In Lemma
12
, we show that near perfect work is drawn only if
\bar{\kappa}\in[0,1]
.

•

Lemma
12
gives us the analytical expression and minimization range in order to evaluate
W_{\textup{ext}}
, according to Eq. (
230
). In Section
E.2.5
, we show how one can evaluate this optimization problem, by comparing the stationary points and endpoints of the function
\frac{\alpha B_{\alpha}}{\alpha-1}
that gives the leading term in Eq. (
230
). Lemma
13
proves a technical property of the first derivative of this function. Using it, we prove in Lemma
14
that one can always choose
\varepsilon(g)
with some
\bar{\kappa}<1
such that the infimum of
\frac{\alpha B_{\alpha}}{\alpha-1}
is obtained at either
\alpha=\bar{\kappa}
or
\alpha\rightarrow\infty
.

•

Finally, in Section
E.2.6
, we use the results in Section
E.2.5
regarding the evaluation of
W_{\textup{ext}}
to find the efficiency in the quasi-static limit.

E.2.1
An explicit expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}

Our first task is to work out an explicit expression for
W_{\textup{ext}}
depending on the initial and final states of the cold bath,
\varepsilon
and hot bath (inverse) temperature
\beta_{h}
. Such as expression is found by applying the generalized second laws as detailed in Section
B.2
.

Lemma 9
.

Consider the transition

\tau_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}\otimes\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}\rightarrow\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\otimes\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}\quad\textup{with}\quad\varepsilon>0.

(167)

where
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}
and
\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}
are defined in Eqs. (
41
), (
42
) respectively. Let
W_{\rm ext}
denote the
maximum
possible value such that Eq. (
167
) is possible via catalytic thermal operations, with a thermal bath of inverse temperature
\beta_{h}
. Let
\beta_{c}>\beta_{h}
.
Then the final state
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}=\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\prime}|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{i}|_{\textup{Cold}}\;
is block-diagonal in the energy eigenbasis, and

\displaystyle W_{\rm ext}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}\penalty\ W_{\alpha},

(168)

\displaystyle W_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta_{h}(\alpha-1)}[\ln(A-\varepsilon^{\alpha})-\alpha\ln(1-\varepsilon)],

(169)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha}q_{i}^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\prime\alpha}q_{i}^{1-\alpha}},

(170)

where
p_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta_{c}E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta_{C}}}
,
q_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta_{h}E_{i}}}{Z_{\beta_{h}}}
, and
p_{i}^{\prime}
are the probability amplitudes of state
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
when written in the energy eigenbasis of
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
.
The quantities
W_{1}
and
W_{\infty}
are defined by taking the limit
\alpha\rightarrow 1,+\infty
respectively.

Proof.

Eq. (
55
) is necessary and sufficient for Eq. (
167
) to be satisfied. We can apply the additivity property of the Rényi divergence, to Eq. (
55
) to find

D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{0}\|\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h})+D_{\alpha}(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})\geq D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{W}}^{1}\|\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h})+D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}}),

(171)

where
\tau_{\beta_{h}}
and
\tau_{\textup{W}}^{h}
are the thermal states with Hamiltonians
\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}
and
\hat{H}_{\textup{W}}
respectively, at inverse temperature
\beta_{h}
. From Eq. (
171
) it follows,

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{\alpha-1}\ln\left[\varepsilon^{\alpha}+(1-\varepsilon)^{\alpha}e^{-\beta(E_{k}-E_{j})(1-\alpha)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})-D_{\alpha}(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})

(172)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\alpha-1}\ln A.

(173)

By examining the three cases
\alpha<1
,
\alpha>1
separately, have find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon^{\alpha}+(1-\varepsilon)^{\alpha}e^{-\beta(E_{k}-E_{j})(1-\alpha)}\geq A&\quad\text{ if }\,\alpha<1\\
\varepsilon^{\alpha}+(1-\varepsilon)^{\alpha}e^{-\beta(E_{k}-E_{j})(1-\alpha)}\leq A&\quad\text{ if }\,\alpha>1\end{split}

(174)

where note that
A
is independent of
E_{k}
and
E_{j}
. Now note that the largest value of
E_{k}-E_{j}
in Eq. (
174
), is obtained when the inequalities hold with equality. Thus, since these equations have to hold for all
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
, the largest amount of work we can extract corresponds to the value of
E_{k}-E_{j}
for which Eq. (
174
) holds for all
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
, with an equality for at least one particular value of
\alpha\in[0,\infty]
. In other words
W_{\rm ext}
is given by Eq. (
168
), and
W_{\alpha}
satisfies,

\varepsilon^{\alpha}+(1-\varepsilon)^{\alpha}e^{-\beta W_{\alpha}(1-\alpha)}=A.

(175)

Note that due to the continuity of the Rényi divergences in the neighbourhood of one, this case follows by continuity. Solving Eq. (
175
) for
W_{\alpha}
gives us Eq. (
169
).
∎

As we will see later there exist
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}
such that,
W_{\textup{ext}}
given by Eq. (
168
), has a solution (i.e.
W_{\textup{ext}}>0
) for
any

\varepsilon>0
. We can use this to write down an explicit solution to the maximization problem Eq. (
62
). Using Eqs. (
62
), (
77
) and Lemma
9
, we conclude

\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})=\left(1-\varepsilon+\frac{\Delta C(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})}{\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}W_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})}\right)^{-1}

(176)

where
W_{\alpha}
is given by Eqs. (
169
), (
170
) and recall
\Delta C
can be found in Eq. (
74
).
From Eqs. (
176
), (
169
), (
170
), we see that the optimization problem
\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}}\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
is still a formidable task. In the next section, see will show that we can use the results from Section
D
, to drastically simplify the problem.

E.2.2
An upper bound for the efficiency

Before moving on to solving the nanoscale efficiency explicitly, we will first use the results of Section
D.3
to find upper bounds for the efficiency in the nanoscale regime, in the context of extracting near perfect work (Def.
5
).

Recall how we have discussed in comparing Sections
B.1
and
B.2
, that the solution for the family of free entropies
F_{\alpha}
, in the case of
F_{1}
is simply the non-equilibrium free energy. Therefore, from Lemma
9
, it follows that
W_{1}
is simply the maximum amount of extractable work according to Eq. (
49
). From Eqs. (
65
), (
77
),

\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})=\left(1-\varepsilon+\frac{\Delta C(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})}{W_{1}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})}\right)^{-1}.

(177)

One can now compare Eq. (
177
) with Eq. (
176
), and note that for any
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
W_{1}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})\geq\inf_{\alpha\geq 0}W_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1})
. Therefore, we conclude that for any
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\in\mathcal{S}
,

\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})\leq\eta^{\textup{mac}}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}).

(178)

Eq. (
178
) in conjunction with Lemma
5
has an important consequence. Namely,

\sup_{\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}}\eta^{\textup{nano}}(\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}})\leq 1-\beta_{h}/\beta_{c}

(179)

with equality only if the state
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}
that solves the supremum is the limiting case where it tends to the initial state of the cold bath,
\rho^{1}_{\textup{Cold}}\rightarrow\rho^{0}_{\textup{Cold}}
. Therefore, in order to see whether we can still achieve the Carnot efficiency, we will consider the quasi-static regime in the rest of Section
E
.

E.2.3
Evaluating near perfect work in the quasi-static heat engine

In light of the results from the previous section, we will now calculate the near perfect work
W_{\textup{ext}}
for quasi-static heat engines, i.e. the case where
\varepsilon,g\ll 1
. Specifically, we make the following assumption about the cold bath Hamiltonian:

(A.5)
The Hamiltonian is taken to be of
n
qubits:

\hat{H}_{\textup{Cold}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mathbbm{1}}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes\hat{H}_{\textup{c},k}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}^{\otimes(n-k)},\quad\textup{where}\quad\hat{H}_{\textup{c},k}=\bar{E}_{k}|\bar{E}_{k}\rangle\langle\bar{E}_{k}|,

(180)

and
\bar{E}_{k}>0
is the energy gap of the
k
-th qubit. Here for simplify, we have chosen w.l.o.g. the ground state of each qubit to have an eigenvalue equal to zero.

The tensor product structure in Assumption (A.5) allows us to simplify
\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}
, to

\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\tau_{i,\beta_{c}},

(181)

where
\tau_{i,\beta_{c}}
is the thermal state of
i
th qubit Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{i,\textup{c}}
at inverse temperature
\beta_{c}
.
For the simplicity of following proofs, we present them in the special case of identical qubits, i.e. that
\bar{E}_{i}=E
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. This means Eq. (
181
) can be reduced to

\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{0}=\tau_{\beta_{c}}^{\otimes n}.

(182)

Furthermore, since we consider quasi-static heat engines, the output state is

\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}=\tau_{\beta_{f}}^{\otimes n},

(183)

with
\beta_{f}=\beta_{c}-g
,where
0<g\ll 1
. Eq. (
180
) together with Eq. (
183
) allows us to further simplify Eq. (
170
) to

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\alpha}q_{i}^{1-\alpha}}{\sum_{i}p_{i}^{\prime\alpha}q_{i}^{1-\alpha}}\right)^{n},

(184)

where
p_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta_{c}E\delta_{i,0}}}{Z_{\beta_{c}}}
,
p_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{e^{-\beta_{f}E\delta_{i,0}}}{Z_{\beta_{f}}}
,
q_{i}=\frac{e^{-\beta_{h}E\delta_{i,0}}}{Z_{\beta_{h}}}
, with
\delta_{1,0}=0,

\delta_{1,1}=1,
are the probabilities of thermal states (different temperatures) for the qubit Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\textup{c}}
.
The proof follows along the same lines as the proof to Lemma
9
, but now noting that in Eq. (
171
) we can replace
D_{\alpha}(\tau_{\textup{Cold}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
and
D_{\alpha}(\rho_{\textup{Cold}}^{1}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
with
nD_{\alpha}(\tau_{\beta_{c}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
and
nD_{\alpha}(\tau_{\beta_{f}}\|\tau_{\beta_{h}})
